ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U263	Our tenth item on the agenda today is the first reading of the draft Adult Education Act 798 initiated by the Government of the Republic. Evgeni Ossinovski, Minister of Education and Science of the Chair, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U264	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Dear Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft Adult Education Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. The current Adult Education Act is one of the oldest comprehensive legislation in force since 1993. However, the training market and life have evolved so much that the current regulation has become obsolete and ineffective. For example, the law requires that most continuing education institutions apply for a training permit to carry out their activities. Applications for training permits shall be processed by the Ministry of Education or by the relevant Training Authorisation Commission. This is a rather voluminous procedure in terms of administrative burden, the positive impact of which is very difficult to assess as the country has no substantive capacity to effectively control the continuing training institutions. Consequently, we have proposed a new regulation to the legislator, where the current system of training licences has been replaced by a different system. In general, in the case of in-service training institutions wishing to provide further education based on the curriculum and to obtain certain benefits, in particular income tax refunds and, on the other hand, study leave for students, the requirement to register in the national education information system would apply. It would have been registered what training is offered, where it is offered, what curricula are, who are trainers and what the output is desired to achieve. It also requires that in-service training institutions have a website where all relevant information has been published. At present, the information on continuing training institutions and on the additional training they provide is extremely uneven. Very often, students face serious difficulties in understanding exactly what the institution offers and what the expected results of this training are. At the same time, the obligation to apply for a licence would remain for trainers providing training in areas of particular importance to the State or to a large extent regulated, for example, drivers' trainers, security staff, train drivers and some others. Regulation is designed in such a way that, in these specific cases, the Ministry of Education cooperates with the ministries in charge of the fields. For example, in the case of motor vehicle driver training, we cooperate with the Road Administration appointed by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications to supervise. Security personnel training is subject to a security law, and the Ministry of the Interior has imposed this supervision on the Police and Border Guard Board. The new registration and authorisation regime would enter into force on 1 July 2016. Thus, a certain transitional period would be possible for trainers to decide whether they will continue to carry out further training without complying with the requirements of the new law, i.e. not registered, meaning that their students will not receive income tax refunds and study leave, or whether they will bring their activities into line with the new law and register their curricula and activities. The draft aims to create the possibility of taking student loans in higher education even if they are part-time. This will make it easier for adult learners to obtain higher education at the appropriate pace with the workload. In addition, the age limit on receiving health insurance will be removed: the draft provides for health insurance for all holders of basic education and general secondary education, regardless of age. At present, the age limit is 21 years for primary education, 24 years for secondary education. For adults who are in education and training, the possibility of taking study leave from work will remain. So much for this bill in general. It's been working hard to develop for a long time. If you look at the draft coordination table, which is quite substantial, you will get an idea of how much work has been done. As part of this draft, we have also included activities arising from the programme of action of the Government of the Republic concerning the quality of Estonian language learning. In this context, the Language Act and the Citizenship Act are also amended: stricter requirements are imposed on providers of Estonian language training. In future, the organiser of the Estonian language refresher training will apply for a licence and the Language Inspectorate will be able to check the level of courses that are basically preparatory to the Estonian language proficiency test. Currently, the providers of Estonian language courses are not supervised on the private market, so learners do not know whether the training they choose actually helps to acquire the official language. We know cases where people have spent their time and money to attend Estonian language courses, but their language skills have not improved significantly since then. This has been the case in the language inspectorate's practice. They have found that a person attends courses, after a year he or she will be tested for language skills and remain the same. It has also been established that there are also Estonian language courses where trainers themselves do not speak Estonian at the C1 level. As a result, it is also planned to introduce a requirement for the Estonian language trainer, which seems elementary in itself, but in practice it has become clear that there is a need for it. After this change has been implemented, the trainer must also record the objective of his/her courses according to language proficiency levels, i.e. the trainer must state the expected level of language proficiency of the learners after the course has been completed. The reason why Estonian language courses go under a stricter procedure under this draft as one of the priority areas is, on the one hand, that improving Estonian language skills is certainly an important national priority. On the other hand, the training of the Estonian language is quite largely funded by the public sector. The language learning programme existed in the previous period of euro money and the new programme for the next period is already being prepared by the Ministry of Education. The law also provides for compensation for those who perform the citizenship sex. So it is also a question of the targeted use of public money. The state is currently compensating for coursework costs or ordering courses for which we cannot be sure that their quality is decent. The Language Inspectorate will have additional powers to carry out this verification and will also receive the necessary additional funding. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U273	I'll start with the last one. Whether this will lead to longer training or simply reduce the ambition of curricula and the degree of willingness of students to undergo longer refresher training, I cannot say that. The reference is the same system of coordination for skills development. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U286	No more questions for the waitress. Thank you very much. I would like a co-presentation by Tiina Lokk-Tramberg, Member of the Cultural Committee of the Chair.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U287	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to thank you very much. The Cultural Commission discussed the draft Adult Education Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic, 798 at its meeting on 8 December. The draft was presented by Evgeni Ossinovski, Minister of Education and Science, and Terje Haidak, Head of the Adult Education Department, who gave an overview of how to change the continuing training system. A distinction shall be made between training providers on the basis of authorisation and notification. A single system of regulation will be set up: regardless of the form of ownership of the training provider, it must meet certain conditions for the benefit of the State to be extended to its trainers, such as reimbursement of income tax on training costs. The rules for granting study leave shall be specified, the responsibility and supervision of the training institution shall be laid down. The impact of the proposal should be such that in the future the national education information system will have a more accurate overview of training providers. And if they are adequately monitored, the quality of training should also improve. Several changes concern requirements for providers of the Estonian language course and the right of the Language Inspectorate to supervise the quality of the Estonian language course. During the debate, it was clarified that the law applies to a large extent to continuing adult education. General provisions refer to the level of education for adults only in order to create a general framework for adult education. Basically, adult education is regulated in each relevant Level Education Act. The terms 'further training' and 'further training' were discussed. According to the explanations, the law regulates, in particular, the provision of training, so it is more appropriate to use the training-centred term 'additional training' rather than the learner-centred term 'additional training'. It was clarified that the requirement for a licence for trainers offering Estonian language courses will enter into force on 30 June 2016, the year after the entry into force of the Act. This is because the current range of persons offering language courses is very diverse and a sufficient transitional period is necessary for the harmonised requirement to enter into force. It was decided to propose that the draft be put on the plenary agenda by 17 December 2014 and that Tiina Lokk-Tramberg be appointed rapporteur, as well as to propose that the first reading be closed and that the deadline for amendments be set at 5.15 p.m. on 6 January 2015. All decisions were taken by consensus. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U291	Ladies and gentlemen, We have before us a good and necessary law, a draft law on adult education. It's been done for a long time. In this sense, I was even slightly sorry that the Minister introduced this draft in the legal side, but missed the opportunity to explain why adult education in Estonia is a fundamental issue. I'll bring these concerns out myself, I believe they're common to all of us. According to the latest census, we have 40,000 people among the people aged 20 to 34 who have only basic education. I repeat: there are 40,000 young people in the 20 to 34 age group who have only basic education. A third of Estonians have no professional education at all, i.e. they have not studied any specialty in any educational institution. Among them are a third more men than women. This sad statistic reminds me of a statement from Mati Heidmetsa. Two years ago, he pointed out in the Estonian Human Development Report that the formation of the so-called educational Lumpen, i.e. the constant increase in the number of people who do not have any professional education, either with or without comprehensive school education, is the biggest obstacle to the progress of the Estonian labour market and the economy. Without any eulogy, one in five boys in essence will either be confined to basic education or will disappear from the basic education system. Each year, depending on the year, the figures are slightly different from the figures of 300-350 boys and girls who disappear from primary school and we do not really know what happens to them. Therefore, the whole Group of the Fatherland and Res Publica Alliance is of the opinion that much more attention must be paid to the issues that this law regulates, i.e. how to attract as many people as possible to further adult learning. But it's only one side of the medal. There is a very serious need to address the issue of motivation and interest in education even before such a coercive situation is reached. But in order not to get the wrong signal out of here, I stress that it is extremely important to stress the attitude of the state that we are not only talking about young people or older people who have only basic education or are not completing any other, higher-level education in adult education. We are also talking about higher education, master's degree, doctorate people who should train themselves further in the labour market. Learning is a lifelong process. And here we come to these questions, which were also discussed in the Committee on Culture. This draft law speaks of trainers with greater emphasis and therefore has such a title. However, a more serious solution would be a comprehensive education code based on the education strategy adopted, as well as on the views and interests of the learner. We now have all the laws governing education based on the form of the educational institution, and this law happens to be based on the age of the learner. If we look around, it doesn't really matter if there's a person who's passed 18 years of age or if he's one of the 400 or so minors we have at the unemployment fund on account of either falling out of primary school or graduate, but he hasn't continued his studies for the next level of education. Let us be honest, about 400 minors at the unemployment fund are not a mark of honour on the chest of the Republic of Estonia. If we look at what can be done better on the opposition side, we will probably not have a lot of proposals when we dissect this bill by individual sections. It's a relatively good bill and well prepared. Congratulations to the Ministry of Education and Research, who prepared the draft! However, we are not satisfied with the fact that the education strategy adopted and the creation of a more comprehensive education space foreseen in it are essentially being blocked. We very much hope that all the questions that will arise in future education laws will be looked at, first and foremost, from the learner's point of view as well as from how the whole educational space will be arranged in such a way that, without administrative coercion and also special flattery, people will be willing and willing to continue learning and, if necessary, to re-learn. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U293	We will move on to the 11th item, which is the first reading of the draft law amending the Basic School and Upper Secondary Schools Act and the Private Schools Act, which was initiated by the Government of the Republic. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U294	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Dear Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft law amending the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act and the Private School Act, which has been initiated by the Government of the Republic. The draft can be broadly divided into three parts. Firstly, there are some more technical points. For example, according to practice, the law provides that direct contracts are concluded between the Ministry of Education and private schools to cover the costs of the labour costs of private school teachers and some other component of the education allowance. The implementation of one point in the programme of action of the Government of the Republic can be called a more fundamental change. This will make the use of education support more flexible in local governments. In particular, it is proposed to allow a component of the investment component or education support which could be used for investment in school infrastructure, where possible and at the request of the local government and the school, to finance the support specialist service or to use these resources to cover other general education costs. Of course, this does not solve all the problems associated with the financing of support professionals. Support specialists are primarily schoolkeepers, in most cases to finance the local government; this service is for them to organise. The state has provided the necessary support so that schools can also order this service from the state Rajasidja centres that started operations in September of this year, but funding remains on the local government. It is also proposed to add two additional funding components in the framework of education support, one relating to the availability of regional upper secondary school education and the other to the learning of Estonian language. The first of these concerns municipalities where the Estonian and Russian upper secondary schools are joined. Due to the decrease in the number of students in so many cities, this issue has either risen or is rising, and it is generally reasonable for Estonian and Russian children to study together. According to feedback from such schools, they need additional support to provide Russian-speaking students with either additional courses or the necessary support for Estonian language learning. We have planned a 10% increase in the upper secondary school teacher component or coefficient in the municipalities where these upper secondary schools are combined. As there are few such municipalities, the overall impact is not very high, with memory estimated at around EUR 60000. The second additional component is intended for municipalities in whose territory one upper secondary school is located, one upper secondary school part. This means that the upper secondary school network in the municipality has been organised as much as it has been able to do, i.e. there is no more than one upper secondary school in the municipality, the next step would be to give up upper secondary school and the number of students will be between 50 and 200. It is understandable that the concentration of upper secondary school education in county centres is quite inevitable because of the decline in the number of students, especially upper secondary school students, and this process is taking place in itself. The role of the state in this process is reflected in the creation and creation of state secondary schools in the county centres. On the other hand, it will certainly take time to set up state secondary schools. The aim is to have a state secondary school in each county centre by 2020. At the same time, issues relating to the provision of upper secondary school education, the provision of quality education, including the issue of hiring teachers, are already topical and problematic in many smaller upper secondary schools. In this context, it is reasonable to offer smaller municipalities where the upper secondary school network has been organised as much as it has been managed, but where the number of students has not fallen below the absolute critical limit, i.e. less than 50 students, a little extra support so that they can provide upper secondary school education better, especially for as long as state secondary schools are established. Although I have to admit that many upper secondary schools outside county centres will probably continue after that, especially in those municipalities where there are more than 50 kilometres to the county centre. There are a few of these in Estonia, and they also want to receive upper secondary school education. That's all about the bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U354	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Minister, thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Members of the Riigikogu! We are now discussing the basic school and upper secondary school law again, this time also the private school law as an addition. When we open the bill, it seems at first glance that § 82, this long-awaited financing model, has come before us. Moreover, the keywords'support specialists' and 'additional support for upper secondary school students' may be added as a bonus even to the single funding of schools of Russian-speaking and Estonian-speaking students are quite positive signs. The fact that the funding of the Private Schools Act has been added is, at first glance, delightful in the light of the Supreme Court's judgment, after all, a quick reaction has been made. However, if we read the draft properly and follow the discussion, we understand that Bill 799 does not solve anything, that none of these issues are financed by private schools, that there is no under-funding for upper secondary school, that there is no additional support for a Russian-speaking student, be it in Estonian school or in the so-called 60:40 school, or the joint curriculum in school, and, unfortunately, that does not solve anything for support specialists. In principle, quite a number of people in the debate in the Committee on Culture were very clear that something could perhaps be done, but if this draft 799 is not adopted, then nothing will happen either. Let me introduce you to the bill. Support specialists have been identified as part of the first. We have been talking for several years about the decision of the previous government to stop funding support specialists within the education allowance provided by the state was a very serious mistake. This has led to a very heavy lay-off of support specialists in several municipalities, or their funding has remained so low, with a workload of either half or a quarter. It is clear that this has a very long educational policy effect. We have a lot of measures taken to make us notice. We are talking about early notice, we are talking about supporting the learning process, we are talking about regular learning of multilingual children, the need for additional support, not to mention the special educational needs in the broadest sense. We have learned to discover different forms of autism, we are talking more and more about hidden Asperger syndrome. We are talking about students with a wide range of special needs who come to a regular school who need additional support. The joint decision was that we must come back to funding support professionals in the new budget. Moreover, there were very high hopes, and that was also the reason, one of the greatest reasons why we sat at night, on very white nights, in the Riigikogu with the social democrats, a little more than a year ago. Now the government coalition has presented us with a draft, where the only measure proposed is that this money can be paid to the extent of the investment component. That won't solve the problem. This is a very small amount, and in most municipalities it is not even a very small amount close to what is actually being spent on support professionals. At least there would be so much flexibility that it could be spent on education support, but there was no such courage. Second topic: private schools. The Bill amends § 22 (2) of the Private Schools Act. The Supreme Court also said that this provision has to be reviewed in principle, because this amendment to the Private School Act and the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act a few years ago, that the so-called box cost has to be paid by the local government, is not justified. In terms of education, we have talked about this repeatedly, it is not justified in the long term. The local government's involvement in private education issues is currently non-existent. Nor can a local government plan a school network if it does not know what is going on in private education. We are certainly opposed to the fact that private education, which has already been born, is now not funded by this decision. The only model that has been proposed is that next year local authorities will continue to finance and that everyone will then go to the court on a compulsory basis and ask the Ministry of Education for their money back through the court. I am very convinced that the Supreme Court, when they suggested that this model should be changed, did not have such a vision in mind that all local authorities automatically go through the court and ask for the state's money back. Probably the Supreme Court still meant that the Riigikogu, which is the legislative power, will work out the model together with the executive power. And three of these models have been submitted to the Committee on Culture. It is certainly possible to propose a model here in the Riigikogu before 1 March or, let's say politely, before the anniversary of independence, a model that works and can be accepted, giving a clear signal to both local authorities and private schools. This model is very simple. This model is the same as that stated in § 22 (2): Private education contracts are concluded by the state with a private school holder. It will also have to pay the so-called box cost to the extent that it is agreed between the state and the local private school holder, and it will be possible to give him a mandate to specify in his regulations how it will be done. There is no need to invent a bicycle or discuss it for months. How exactly is going to proceed, for example, on the weighted average, which options will be available for controlling the quality of private education in the future, this is already the place of supervision procedure, and this will certainly be dealt with in more detail by the next Riigikogu. And last of high school education. It is very sad that the second government is taking steps backwards. Looking at the countries that set an example for us, even in Finland nearby, upper secondary school education, with electives, non-electives, which teach several foreign languages, is financed almost one and a half times more than basic education. We have very underfunded upper secondary school education. According to the last regulation of January last year, it is even more underfunded, sometimes 30% less than primary education. This isn't the right direction. These are the reasons why the Centre Party Group does not support the further processing of draft 799, proposing that the procedure be suspended and the draft be returned to the Ministry. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U355	Thank you. I ask for the speech chair of the Riigikogu, representative of the Social Democratic Party Group Jaak Allik!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U356	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Group of the Social Democratic Party supports the draft amendment to the PGS presented to us, seeing it as a first, albeit timid, step on the right track. To do this, we need to develop a model for funding the school network, where the state, i.e. the Ministry of Education, and local authorities go in the same way. One of the virtues of the draft we are currently discussing is that for more than several years the proposed solution has received the coordination of the local government associations. In the meantime, the whip and carrot method, which forced only a uniform school network with large and clean state gymnasiums, and hindered the development and financing of so-called full cycle schools in rural areas, has never been acceptable to social democrats. And it's not only that bending the entire school network into uniform force is pointless, but it's damaging. Rumours that only in a large and multi-choice upper secondary school will the student receive a high-quality education have not been confirmed in practice, whether by analysing the rankings of the state exams or by considering the long-standing international level of Estonian education. It is also a matter of taking life force from many Estonian rural settlements and small towns through the semi-punitive closure of rural secondary schools. Finally, it is also the case that the so-called "big and clean state gymnasium" is far from fit for everyone who wants to continue their education in high school. I will give an example of my country, Viljandi, where the maximum capacity of the state gymnasium is 180 students on the flight. They are now also filled, with only a dozen students studying there from outside Viljandi. At the same time, there are four more cycle schools in the county, in Suure-Jaani, Tartus, Abjas and Karksi-Nuia, where more than 80 students are studying in one flight. What right and basis would these students have to take up the possibility of upper secondary school education? It is therefore very commendable that the proposed legislative amendment provides for the possibility for the state to provide additional support for upper secondary schools, where the number of students in the secondary school is between 50 and 200. Of course, this support is still too small and does not guarantee that state secondary schools and municipal secondary schools are treated equally, as provided for by the Constitution. Additional funding is also required for secondary schools where children from Estonian and Russian-speaking comprehensive schools study together. A symbolic step will be taken in the draft by extending the national wage subsidy to support specialists. At least with this, we note at law level that it is also the duty of the state to take care of support specialists. In the meantime, this was done at the level of a government regulation that promised to pay them a salary on the account of education support. In the past few years, however, the Ministry of Education tried to ignore the problem. At the same time, while increasing the number of HEV students in regular schools and closing HEV1 schools, it has been completely illogical and irresponsible at national level to bypass the funding of support specialists, even though the law prescribes the existence of these services in schools. The current possibility that support specialists may be paid for the investment component is certainly not a sufficient solution, especially given that in larger centres, such as Tallinn, Tartu, Viljandi and elsewhere, the entire investment component is actually required to repay loans taken to repair school buildings. Of course, it is positive in itself that the investment component of the education grant remained at all, because a year ago, it was probably also under attack. The real solution to the problem is that within the overall education allowance, there is a clear amount of money that goes to hire support professionals or to buy the corresponding service from the Pathfinder centres. We hope to see that in next year's budget. Thank you.

In order to mitigate the potential risk of security talk, we will see quite clear arrangements in our draft this time. Such purchase and sale could only take place with the permission of the county governor, who is obliged to ask the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of the Interior for an opinion on each particular transaction. One to secure that this hut with the plot is not an object of national defence, and the other to look into the buyer's background. Why do we pursue it? Firstly, because this is a practical problem, as demonstrated by the frequent raising of this issue at meetings with the voters of Ida-Virumaa. The point is that there is no sufficient market for such immovable property among the citizens of the Republic of Estonia, but these houses should not fall apart and the land should be fertilely used. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U418	We will move on to the 14th item, which is the first reading of the draft Act amending the Local Government Organisation Act and the Local Government Election Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like to ask Hanno Pevkuri, Minister of the Interior, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U419	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! This draft is certainly a technical draft on the one hand, but it is nevertheless a good draft. The main objective of the draft before you is to solve the problems raised in the elections of local government units and councils, as well as the problems raised in the everyday practice of the Riigikogu Election Committee and under the attention of the Supreme Court. Problems relate to the early termination of the council member's mandate, the appointment of an alternate council member and the performance of the functions of the city and rural municipality secretary. The draft also specifies the content of the operating limit for a council member and the language requirement for the mayor, mayor and a member of the government. The inconsistencies between the provisions in force so far and the resulting ambiguities will also be clarified. The following are details of the changes. Firstly, in the future, personal elections will not be subject to the operating restrictions laid down in the Anti-Corruption Act. Secondly, according to the practice of the Supreme Court, it is stated that a member of the local council, who is at the same time an official in the same municipality, is not subject to the premature termination of the mandate of a council member, but his powers stop until the person is a member of the city or rural municipality government. In the event of the termination of the suspension of the mandate of a member of the Council, the person shall have the right to choose whether he wishes to continue as an official or as a member of the council. Thirdly, an alternate shall be provided with the option of choosing whether he wishes to become a member of the Council or to continue his work until now if his former office cannot be reconciled with his status as a member of the Council. Until now, the alternate council member's mandate was terminated before the deadline and the alternate had no choice. Fourthly, a member of a council elected or appointed or appointed, in the same municipality or city, as an elder or mayor of the municipality mayor may no longer participate in the discussion or decision on an individual act conferring remuneration or compensation, even if the suspension of the mandate of his or her member of the council has not yet entered into force. The purpose of the provision is to make it clear that the economic interests of the relevant council member are in this case. Fifthly, it is stated that the results of the personal elections will not be voted on publicly. Sixthly, the language requirement for an elder, mayor or member of the government is specified. The language requirement is also specified for local government officials and employees. However, it must be acknowledged that this is not a new requirement at the level of the law, since it concerns those persons who were excluded from the scope of that law when the new Civil Service Act entered into force. Seventhly, it is specified that the president of the paid council, the mayor and the mayor who has been dismissed as a result of a change in the administrative-territorial organisation shall be compensated by a decision of the council. Eighthly, it is stipulated that, if a replacement has been appointed for the absence of a rural municipality or city secretary, the functions of chairman of the municipal and city electoral committees shall be performed by a replacement of a rural municipality or city secretary. This change is due to the observation of the Supreme Court. Ninthly, the Local Government Council Election Act provides for a deadline of five working days for a candidate who has been elected to the Council, who is at that time in a position incompatible with the membership of the Council, to report his or her choice to the Election Commission. The draft proposes to harmonise the notification period with the Local Government Organisation Act by setting out three working days. When the bill is adopted, Estonia's legal space will hopefully become clearer. The decisions of the Supreme Court are introduced into the Estonian legal order and the different interpretations of legislation are also reduced. Thank you.

A parish elder or mayor is appointed by a council or city council and nothing changes as regards its appointment. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U429	The 15th item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Aliens Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and in this connection the amendment of other laws. It is a great honour and pleasure to welcome once again Hanno Pevkur, Minister of the Interior, in the Riigikogu Chair. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U430	Mr. Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! The vice-president of the Riigikogu has already read the title of the law to you, so I will go straight to the content. The draft is quite substantial, including amendments to a total of eight laws, so I can only discuss my report on the most important groups of amendments. Firstly, a transitional period of 90 days will be introduced after the end of the residence permit, during which an alien may stay in Estonia and apply for the permit on a new basis. Foreign students and researchers or academics who graduated from Estonian higher education institutions will have an even longer transition period, 183 days in particular. The purpose of the amendment is to create the possibility of a flexible transition from one temporary residence permit to another. The distinction between students, researchers and lecturers is also due to the draft directive on learning and research migration, which is being discussed in the Council of Europe working group, which provides for the retention of researchers and students for a maximum period of six months. It aims to contribute to human capital of high-skilled potential workers in Europe in order to foster research development and innovation. Secondly, a new basis for granting a residence permit is established for moving to Estonia permanently. And it is given to a person who has lived in Estonia for at least three years and has adapted here. The purpose of creating this type of residence permit is to enable foreigners who have or have had a residence permit for the last five years to stay in Estonia, regardless of whether the alien has in the meantime settled in another country or have stayed in Estonia. Thirdly, there is the possibility of mitigating structural labour shortages in a sector determined by the government, by paying the employee an average salary in at least the relevant sector. The amendment stipulates that the government may establish a list of areas where there is a high labour shortage and where there is no labour force in the Estonian labour market with the required skills, qualifications and work experience. Fourthly, it is stipulated that the unemployment fund may authorise the recruitment of several aliens to Estonia at the same time, instead of one former foreigner, if the corresponding skills and skilled workforce cannot be found in Estonia. At present, the unemployment fund grants permission to fill a job with a specific person. The amendment clarifies the regulation of the unemployment fund's authorisation requirement and states that the unemployment fund may authorise several jobs at the same time and does not relate to a specific alien, since it is not the responsibility of the unemployment fund to assess the specific situation of the foreigner but of the workforce in a particular area. Fifthly, it is stipulated that an alien temporarily or temporarily staying in Estonia on the basis of a temporary residence permit has the possibility to work as a temporary agency worker in Estonia. Sixthly, it is stipulated that an alien who has come to work in Estonia can work for several employers at the same time. The amendment removes the differences that require the permission of the Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund or the payment of the salary provided for in the Act when starting work with another employer, as in reality working with an alien does not affect the situation of the Estonian labour market, but at the same time reduces the possibilities for an alien to contribute to Estonian society. In addition, a legislative amendment will help prevent unreasonable situations where, for example, an alien working as a scientist is prohibited from publishing an article in a scientific journal because it is working with another employer within the meaning of the Aliens Act. Seventhly, the regulation on digital ID issuance of e-resident is being supplemented, which, as we know, entered into force already on 1 December and has received wide-ranging attention in the world. The draft provides a legal basis for issuing digital IDs to an e-resident on the basis of a single customer contact and for applying for and issuing digital IDs through foreign agencies. The current regulation does not allow the e-resident to issue a document via foreign representations. In order to simplify the process of issuing digital IDs for e-resident and to create convenient conditions for applying, the corresponding additions to the Identity Documents Act are planned. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! As a member of the government, I certainly support the proposal of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs to conclude the first reading of the draft and to adopt the draft as a law as soon as possible so that we can get on with these changes in life. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U431	Minister, thank you. No questions. I would like Mr Andres Herkel, member of the Constitutional Committee, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U436	We will move on to the 16th item, which is the first reading of the draft Act amending the Advertising Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask Urve Palo, Minister of Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U437	Good evening, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, Members of the Riigikogu! The draft law amending the Advertising Act is part of a package of measures to address the problems encountered in the credit market. In addition to the advertising restrictions imposed by this draft, the following measures are included in the wider package: A draft has been prepared at the Ministry of Finance which imposes an authorisation obligation on non-bank creditors and credit intermediaries and all creditors and credit intermediaries are subject to supervision by the Financial Supervision Authority. The Ministry of Justice's draft bans the resolution of disputes arising from consumer credit agreements in arbitration and sets a ceiling on the percentage of charge for consumer credit agreements. It also limits the collection costs charged to the consumer and clarifies the rules on late payment. All these drafts have now also reached the Riigikogu proceedings and I hope that they will move forward quickly. Limiting consumer credit advertising is an important part of the whole package of measures. Unfortunately, the promotion of consumer credit by businesses so far has given rise to a view in society that borrowing is a natural part of life, regardless of the person's ability to repay the loan at a later stage. The recognised fact is that advertising and its content significantly shape the information space in which people live. Consumer credit advertising constantly recalls the possibility of financing a loan as one of its daily needs and therefore normalises it as a solution. As of 28 July of this year, 35976 persons who have been registered in the Register of Disorders of Payments who have been in default on loans issued by companies specialising in the granting of consumer credit. It's more than people living in Pärnu. Of these, 56% had one loan up as a default, while the remaining 44%, or half, had two or three or even more loans. Unfortunately, according to debt advisors, the number of problem borrowers is increasing as time moves. This draft is designed with the aim of creating a healthier information environment where the consumption of products potentially dangerous to people's economic lives is not considered to be self-evident, thereby protecting the interests and rights of the weaker party in the contractual relationship. The main changes to meet this objective are as follows: Firstly, the information disclosed in the advertising of consumer credit, including after-tax, leasing, mortgage and consumer credit, is limited. According to the amendment, consumer credit advertising may in the future contain only essential information provided by law, such as the name and registration number of the service provider, site contact details, the example of the percentage rate of charge, the call for access to financial services conditions, the repayment period and similar information. Secondly, the advertising of consumer credit, namely the promotion of the loan of money in the television and radio programme, is prohibited. Advertising in print and outdoor media and on the Internet will remain acceptable, but with the restrictions I spoke about in advance, i.e. only the name of the service provider, the licence number, the contact details of the site, the example of the percentage rate of charge and the call for access to the conditions of financial services can be shown. Consumer credit promotion must be responsible, it must not give the impression that it is an opportunity to solve financial problems, and it must not tip off reckless borrowing. I would point out that the prohibition does not apply to advertising of housing loans, credit cards used as cross-border means of payment, hire-purchase or leasing. In the case of advertising broadcasts broadcast in television or radio programmes, the conditions of the promotional product cannot be sufficiently understood and deepened as contact is short-term. This period is not sufficient to allow consumers to make a fully considered decision on the consumption of services, and therefore advertising bans are particularly on TV and radio. A ban on advertising on television and radio does not mean a ban on the disclosure of a trade mark if it has been done on the principle of responsibility, i.e. the trade mark may be advertised but the product may not. Sponsor notifications shall also be accepted. The changes proposed by the draft will result in a reduction in the number of consumer credit borrowers who make the loan decision lightly, without adequately assessing the risks to their economic well-being. This will reduce both the number of people in default and the worsening of the defaults of persons already in debt and the transmission of the related social problems. I very much hope, good members of the Riigikogu, that next Christmas, we will no longer see such ads where we are called upon to take a quick loan in order to buy gifts or have a couch similar to that of a neighbor. Such emotional adverts will disappear from 1 March next year and the number of people in financial difficulties, which has increased due to SMS borrowing, will be significantly reduced. Once again, I stress that, of course, restricting advertising is not the only magic wand, but it is very important that all three ministries, the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Justice and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications have worked together and developed the whole package, which could be of great use to the people of Estonia, I think. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U438	Thank you. Ambassador Arto Aas, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U439	Thank you, Ambassador Ratas! Minister, thank you very much. Does this draft entail certain risks to television channels? We have had concerns that, since this sector accounts for about 10% of the advertising revenue from private television channels, the disappearance of this part could have a significant impact on the quality and availability of their services. Have you been familiar with such consequences or possible negative effects?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U440	Thank you. I have also read articles of opinion on this subject and have listened to comments. The truth is that the proposed changes have a certain impact on the profitability of media companies. Market participants estimate that the share of all loan adverts in the advertising turnover of channels ranges from 4 to 5%. The highest known share reached 9%. I guess that's the maximum, but speaking to market participants, it's generally 41⁄2 percent on average, and it's 9% maximum. This assessment takes into account all loan promotions, but according to the draft, the ban on the channel does not apply to several loan forms, such as leasing, post-payments, housing loans and cross-border credit cards, which is likely to have a lower impact. In addition, I would like to stress here that we need to look at this picture much wider than the profits of some companies. In the same way, you might consider allowing smoke advertising for example on TV. I'm sure it'll give the TV channels extra revenue. But that is not what we are doing, because we understand that it is not responsible to allow cigarettes to be advertised. So we don't even consider it.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U441	Minister, thank you. No further questions. I would like to ask Kristen Michal, member of the Economic Commission, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U442	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I will also give a brief overview of the draft. Since the bill is the same as the executive, or government, has entrusted to the tender hands of the legislators, I apologise in advance for the repetition of the evening! I'm trying to be as brief as possible, but there's still a few things I have to turn down for the verbatim. What's this bill doing? The same draft, as the Minister has already said, briefly limits the advertising of fast-track loans. This bill is part of a larger plan and our spring coalition agreement. This coalition agreement sets out the principle that, in order to protect consumers, the activities of high-speed credit companies will be subject to stricter control, and among the measures that will carry out this verification, the introduction of a licence requirement, effective national supervision and advertising restrictions. So this is what this bill is about. The Minister has already introduced it, but I will once again list the drafts that are part of this larger plan. Firstly, the already mentioned draft law on creditors and intermediaries harmonises the requirements for market operators. The draft Law on Creditors and Credit Intermediaries complements precisely the activities of non-bank creditors and credit intermediaries, i.e. those who grant credit to private individuals, i.e. consumers. Moreover, as has been said, this principle also follows from the Coalition Agreement that it lays down the obligation for them to apply for authorisation, which essentially brings together all creditors and intermediaries under the supervision of the Financial Supervision Authority. Those who are familiar with previous debates on SMS loans know that it has actually been the subject of this debate for several years and is also part of the package. This is not the subject of this draft, but it is part of the package. The second part of the larger plan that the coalition has concerning SMS loans is linked to the Code of Civil Procedure, which prohibits the resolution of disputes arising from consumer credit agreements in arbitration. The Law of Obligations Act and the Law on the General Part of the Civil Code are also amended in order to set a cap on the percentage of charge for a consumer credit agreement. This is the three-fold cost rate of consumer loans published by Eesti Pank in the draft, which is in the calculated order of 100-110%. There is always a question of whether this could be a little more stringent, but the question remains with the Commission. The third part of this package is the draft law amending the Law of Obligations Act and the Tabitor Protection Act, which sets specific limits on the amount of collection costs charged to the consumer and, to some extent, also specifies the regulation of late payment. It also establishes certain differences during the duration of the contractual relationship and after the termination of the contract. Now specifically this draft, which we are talking about, is the draft law amending the advertising law. The law amending and implementing the Law on Law Enforcement, which entered into force in mid-2013, transposed into the Advertising Act a Directive of the European Parliament and of the Council laying down standard information to be included in any advertisement containing an interest rate or any other invoice relating to credit costs to the consumer. This draft is also based on this standard information as regards the information to be disclosed in advertising, but goes beyond the requirements of the Directive by limiting the information to be provided in advertising to the name, registration number and contact details of the service provider. To summarise briefly, it can be said that any adverts that indicate that a person may receive an amount of money or that someone is communicating such contracts are not covered by a mortgage, credit card, hire-purchase or lease-purchase are prohibited in the television and radio programme. Advertising informing that consumer credit is granted or the corresponding contracts are mediated may only provide the following information (predominantly mandatory information as mentioned): the name and registration number of the service provider, the contact details of the place of business, a typical example of credit terms with the information set out in the law, information on interest rates (whether fixed or fixed), fees included in the total cost of the credit, as well as information on whether it is necessary to conclude a side contract (which is then required) in order to obtain the credit or to obtain the credit, as well as an invitation to examine the terms and conditions of financial services and, where appropriate, to consult an expert. So generally, relatively technical advertising comes out and certainly has an impact on loan promotions. As the Minister has also mentioned, I do not think that the members of the Riigikogu, or many others, are pleased that one area must be restricted in the market. But it certainly isn't quite moral and reasonable if we have to listen on radio and TV just before Christmas to a commercial that says Dad, buy me a sled and take a SMS loan. However, to me, as a right-wing, market-believing liberal, this does not seem quite reasonable. So that is why we are in this draft today and that is the text in the coalition agreement. There was a relatively meaningful debate in the Commission. Ministers and experts were asked why the term "high-speed credit' is not defined. The representatives of the Ministry explained that they have cooperated with other ministries for this purpose, but have not been able to find a universal solution and therefore the package has already been chosen. Then it came about why the channels where loan ads are restricted were set up in this way, namely, why television and radio. As the explanatory memorandum to the bill and the Ministry say, the basis for the choice is the fact that the emotional nature of the advertisement can be removed. On television and on radio, loan ads are more emotional, and they are mostly channels where these ads are actually presented in this form. They then asked how such advertisements were regulated in neighbouring countries. And the answer was that the European Union Consumer Credit Directive, which obliges consumers to provide certain information when products are advertised, is, in general, mandatory information that must be included in advertising. And elsewhere, advertising has not been banned in any information channels; in this sense, Estonia is the first case. In addition, there was further discussion on what would happen if these conditions were violated. The liability for the infringement would lie with the advertiser, the carrier and the carrier. It was also pointed out that alcohol, for example, has not caused such problems. Last but not least, the idea of creating a register of persons subject to debt has been theorised. It was said that there is a private payment disruption register in Estonia, where everyone can search out the information of interest. So there is no register of persons as such, but there have been thoughts about it, and then everyone can wonder if they would like it or not. There was no decision on this in the Commission, and I personally think that we will not see such a register of persons with a loan obligation at least soon. Now for procedural decisions. There is a proposal from the Commission to put the draft on the agenda for today, which has also been put forward, and a proposal to close the first reading (the decision was consensual), as well as a proposal to set the deadline for amendments at 3 a.m. on 9 January 2015 (also a consensual decision). Thanks, everybody!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U450	Let's move on to item 17: First reading of the draft law amending traffic law and state fees law initiated by the Government of the Republic. Urve Palo, Minister of Economy and Infrastructure of the Riigikogu, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U451	Dear Vice-Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, The Government of the Republic has submitted to the Riigikogu for adoption a draft law amending the Traffic Act and the State Fees Act. Its main objectives are as follows: Firstly, to implement an action programme of the Government of the Republic to analyse the feasibility of allowing bicycles, mopeds and electric cars to a public transport lane. According to the draft, public transport lanes could also be allowed for electric vehicles in order to popularise their use. Since electric vehicles are only a marginal part, namely 0.12% of vehicles registered in Estonia, the change would not have a significant impact on public transport traffic. At the same time, the right to decide on the admission of bicycles and mopeds to public transport lanes is granted to the road owner. As bicycles and mopeds are slower than public transport, this leads to the need to pass them. It is therefore necessary to decide for each section of the road whether it is safe to allow bicycles and mopeds to a public roadway. This can only be done by the owner of the tea. The second objective is to implement the measures foreseen in the national road safety programme. The draft allows for the introduction of automatic traffic monitoring systems installed on urban roads. Examples include cameras at the intersection of Tallinn Kristiine, which allow to detect speeding, crossings with prohibited traffic lights, and unauthorised use of public transport lanes. In order to process the infringement data, the Road Administration has established the necessary database and the Police and Border Guard Board will handle the infringements. In particular, the costs of maintaining traffic surveillance systems must be borne by the installer. The third objective is to increase road safety for children. According to the current law, a group of children can move on the sidewalk and, in the absence thereof, on the road bed. Since most city streets have footpaths or bicycle and footpaths, children's groups need to move on the road bed. The amendment also allows a group of children to travel on a road or on a bicycle and a road where traffic is, of course, safer. The safety of young children in vehicles without safety belts shall also be enhanced. From now on, a child under 3 years of age may not be carried in a passenger car or truck in a seat without a seat belt. The requirement is necessary to ensure the safety of young children both in the event of an accident and in the case of sudden braking in daily traffic. The fourth draft aims at simplifying the requirements for carrying and applying for documents. An example can be given when driving licences are completed after examination or when driving licences are exchanged: at that time the vehicle can be driven without a driving licence, the entitlement to drive is certified on the basis of the data in the driving register. It will also be possible to prove the right to instruct a person who learns to drive a motor vehicle on the basis of traffic register data in the future and the right to drive a cyclist will not have to carry the document. It is therefore no longer mandatory to carry a driving licence issued to cyclists in Estonia. The use of driving licences issued abroad in Estonia is also facilitated. The requirement for an international driving licence shall be waived if the person's driving licence is accompanied by entries either in Latin or reproduced in that document. The driving licence must also contain information on the vehicles the person is entitled to drive. In addition, in cases where a driving licence does not comply with these requirements, a notarial translation as an alternative to an international driving licence shall be allowed. This allows for driving, for example, when a person has not applied for an international driving licence in his/her home country. The fifth objective is to improve the availability of public services. To this end, the current principle that a fee-paid operation may be performed by an authority only after receipt of the state fee is amended. The current requirement extends, for example, the time of the Road Administration's operations in the traffic register and may lead to misunderstandings in the verification of the receipt of the state fee. The current regime also causes fees that may vary several times when paying with a bank card. According to the bill, the holder of the state fee may perform the necessary act before the receipt of the state fee if the amount of the state fee is booked on the relevant account. It's just like when you pay at a store with a card, you don't have to get the money into the store's account first, and the buyer doesn't have to wait, the grocery basket is in his hand, he can go home. The amendment thus allows for a better public service. Thank you!
