ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U493	We have already reached item 18. This is the first reading of the draft law amending the Rail Law, initiated by the Government of the Republic, 806. Mr Urve Palo, Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, would you like a speech chair?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U494	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Hello again, ladies and gentlemen. The Railway Act proposes the following amendments on the basis of the draft. Firstly, the passenger rights and obligations imposed on rail passengers will be updated. The rights of rail passengers are governed by Regulation No 1371/2007 of the European Parliament and of the Council on rail passengers' rights and obligations, of which Article 2 (4) and (6) allow Member States to introduce temporary derogations. It allows derogations to be granted for a number of articles for a period of five years, which can be extended twice. Article 2 (3) of Regulation (EC) No 1371/2007 further lists the articles to which no derogations may be granted, the requirements of which were applicable in all Member States immediately after its entry into force on 3 December 2009. The amendment updates the existing list of derogations established in Estonia, which was adopted in Estonia five years ago. Firstly, I will talk about which exceptions we are now giving up, and then about which exceptions remain. The amendment waives the derogations concerning the provision of travel information to train passengers (Article 8); informing a disabled person when a train access restriction is applied (Article 20 (2)); ensuring access to the platform, railway station and rolling stock for persons with reduced mobility (Article 21); providing assistance to persons with reduced mobility on board and on board and on boarding and disembarking (Article 23); conditions for granting assistance, including the obligation for persons with reduced mobility to inform at least 48 hours in advance of their need to receive assistance on board the train (Article 24) and the payment of compensation for damage to or loss of assistance to persons with reduced mobility due to the railway undertaking (Article 25). These are the exceptions that we are giving up. The following derogations shall continue to apply to domestic passenger transport: First of all, in accordance with Article 10, I would like to mention the alignment of the travel information and booking system with the technical specifications of the European Union interoperability, as this will require major investments. Next, in accordance with Article 13 (2), an advance payment of EUR 21000 should be paid by the railway undertaking in the event of the death of the passenger, regardless of who was guilty. Article 15 provides that, irrespective of the existence of fault, the railway undertaking must always assume responsibility for the passenger, for example in the event of a delay or missed journey. Pursuant to Article 18 (2), (4) and (5), in the event of delay, free of charge food and drink shall be offered to passengers for up to 60 minutes and, where appropriate, a hotel place. Article 22 imposes an obligation to assist persons with disabilities at railway stations and may require additional staff to be employed. In the case of international passenger transport to third countries, derogations shall apply in respect of Articles 10, 13 (2) and 15, 16, 17 governing the arrangements for reimbursement of the ticket price in the event of delay of one hour or more. There have been delays of more than an hour due to delays in border control from time to time. In short, this complicated regulation. The list of railway workers responsible for railway safety and railway traffic management who are subject to separate health requirements and procedures for carrying out health surveillance shall be specified. The amendment concerns the health requirements of railway workers and concerns the updating and clarification of the wording in the light of the regulation of Commission Decision 2011/314/EU laying down common European Union health standards for some railway workers. One amendment concerns infringement proceedings by the European Commission No 2013/2040 concerning Directive 2004/49/EC of the European Parliament and of the Council on safety on the Community's railways, amending Council Directive 95/18/EC on the licensing of railway undertakings and Directive 2001/14/EC on the allocation of railway infrastructure capacity and the levying of charges for the use of railway infrastructure and safety certification. The latter is the so-called Railway Safety Directive. The European Commission refers to the fact that the safety authority of one Member State cannot withdraw Part A of the safety certificate issued by another Member State. Estonia agrees with the Commission's complaint and this amendment brings the provision into line with Article 10 (5) (4) of Directive 2004/49/EC, which provides that the safety authority shall inform the safety authority which issued Part A of the safety certificate without delay if it withdraws Part B of the safety certificate. In addition, the European Commission refers to the fact that in certain cases, i.e. where the safety authority has become aware of this in the course of its supervision, the safety authority must inform the safety investigation authority of the accident. Estonia agrees with the Commission's complaint and the provision is now brought into line with Article 21 (3) of Directive 2004/49/EC. The last amendment is to establish the duration of the timetable period and the associated deadlines in accordance with Directive 2012/34/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council establishing a single European railway area. There is a problem with the dates of the timetable period, since § 54 (2) of the Railway Act provides for the beginning of the timetable period on the last Sunday of May and, finally, the Saturday preceding the last Sunday of May of the following year. In reality, however, the start of the timetable period depends on how CIS and Baltic infrastructure managers agree on the dates of the timetable period. For example, in 2014, the traffic schedule period started on 1 June. As the duration of the timetable period and the associated time limits are governed by Directive 2013/34/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council establishing a single European railway area, the wording of the railway law is aligned with the regulation set out in Annex VII to that Directive. This regulation allows infrastructure managers, where appropriate, to set their own dates for the start and end of the timetable period. Thank you!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U495	Any questions for the waitress? There are no questions. Thank you very much, Minister Urve Palo. I would like to speak with Deniss Borodichi, Member of the Economic Commission.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U496	Good chairman of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, Good minister! The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed this draft at its meeting on 8 December. The Commission was invited to present the draft by Minister Urve Palo and Indrek Laineveer, Head of the Railways and Roads Department of MKM. Both gave a very thorough overview of this draft, which mainly concerns the transposition of European Union legislation into the Estonian legal area. The content of the draft was understandable to the members of the Commission, but some practical questions arose during the debate. I'll get some of them here. The first question was the State's obligation to passengers in the event of a disruption. The Minister replied that, as a rule, passengers would be offered the opportunity to use other means of transport in the event of a train failure and that compensation would be paid in the event of a delay, regardless of whose fault it happened. According to the statistics provided by Elron, they have 800 exits per month and 99% of them are on time. Compared to Finland and Sweden, we have a significantly lower number of technical failures. The second and more practical question was raised by the Member of the Commission, Jaan Õunapuu, who asked about the monitoring of passenger rights and ticket sales. He gave an example of problems relating to the marking of seats for first-class wagons. As a result of this debate, the Commission supported Mr Õunapuu's proposal that in future the train driver should inform passengers about previously purchased tickets and ask not to occupy purchased seats. Elron will introduce MKM to the operation of the ticket system in January next year. Following this long and substantive debate, the Commission decided to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 17 December and that the first reading be closed. It was also important to decide to propose a deadline for amendments of 5 January 2015 at 12 noon. All decisions were taken by consensus. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U497	Any questions? There are no questions. Thank you very much, Deniss Borodich. Do you want to open negotiations? There's no call. The commission's proposal was to conclude the first reading of draft 806. First reading is over. I will set the deadline for submitting amendments to the draft at 12 noon on 5 January 2015.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U498	Our next item is the first reading of the Act amending the Emergency Act, the Public Transport Act and the State Fees Act, initiated by the Government of the Republic. Once again, I would like to ask the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, Urve Palo!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U499	Dear Vice-Chairman, Good ambassadors! This draft amends three laws. First of all, I mention the Emergency Act, which lays down the functioning of shipping between the Estonian continent and the large islands as a vital service. Secondly, the draft concerns the Public Transport Act, which mainly introduces two types of changes. It adds regulation to allow direct award of contracts for the provision of public service obligations for the provision of passenger transport services in accordance with the Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council. The law also complements the regulation of regular passenger transport licences, as is the case in bus and coach transport. Thirdly, the State Fees Act is amended, supplemented by provisions concerning the obligation to pay the State fee for the examination of path licence applications and changes to timetable applications. It is important to stress that the purpose of the amendment of the Emergency Act is to provide the State with possible emergency measures, i.e. emergency measures. Even then, people must be able to exercise their fundamental rights. The changes are merely 'back-up plans', which we do not consider likely to materialise. At the same time, the State must also be prepared for worst-case scenarios so that specific measures can be taken in the event of an emergency situation. Let me first talk about the main objectives of the amendment of the Emergency Act. Firstly, the operation of passenger ferry traffic between Saaremaa, Muhumaa and Hiiumaa is added to the list of vital services. Pursuant to § 38 (5) of the Emergency Act, § 2 (1) of Regulation No 16 of the Minister of the Interior of 8.06.2010 "Guidelines for the preparation of risk analysis on business continuity', a vital service is a service which is essential for the organisation of vital social activities, health, safety, security and the economic and social well-being of people. According to this definition, ferry connections between Estonia's large islands and the mainland must also be considered a vital service. It is the duty of the State to ensure a consistent, stable, safe, high-quality and economically efficient link between the islands and the continent. The ferry connection is the only way to bring goods both to the islands and to deliver local production to the mainland. Ferry connections are also almost the only way of transporting people, because they can also meet needs that alternative air connections cannot take into account. There are few people on scheduled flights between islands and the mainland, and it is not possible for aircraft to carry cars and larger quantities of goods. Therefore, ferry connections are vital for the preservation of people's freedom of movement as well as of the economic activities of the islands. This is why ferry connections with the islands must be included in the list of vital services, as there are rail transport, including the operation of public passenger transport. Until now, ferry connections were not included in the Emergency Act, while they include the operation of public transport within the municipality or city as a vital service. The operation of the country's main and supporting roads is also referred to as a vital service in the Emergency Act. In view of the logic, the same vital service is the operation of ferry connections between the mainland and the islands. In principle, ferry connections can be considered an extension of state roads and the link between the mainland and the islands is as vital as the main roads of any other country. In order to ensure that ferry connections function, it is important to keep in mind both waterways, ports and passenger ships which, as a whole, ensure ferry connections. Setting the operation of ferry connections between the mainland and the large islands as a vital service also gives public authorities the possibility to intervene in certain exceptional cases in the operation of ferry connections. The provision of ferry services must be continuous, only then will people have the opportunity to realise their fundamental rights. Secondly, I will present the main objectives of the amendment of the Public Transport Act. Firstly, the possibility to conclude direct contracts is established for the provision of services for the carriage of passengers by inland waterway and in the territorial waters of the Republic of Estonia in accordance with Regulation No 1370/2007 of the European Parliament and of the Council. Although the mandatory scope of the Regulation is road and rail traffic, it provides for the possibility that it may also be applied in water traffic and is optional for the country. The right to conclude direct contracts in ferry traffic, as stated in Article 5 of that regulation, is laid down. The direct award of a contract is the award of a public service contract under Article 1 of the Regulation to a particular public service provider without prior competitive tendering. Direct contracts allow passenger transport between the Estonian islands and the mainland to be organised in a more flexible and stable manner. As a result, keeping in touch does not depend solely on the success of the procurement procedure. However, it will be applied in exceptional cases where the procurement procedure fails for any reason or it is not reasonable to organise it in the light of market conditions. Direct contracts can be concluded with a legally separate entity over which the competent authority exercises control, i.e. with a controlled company of the State or local government. The "in-house" transactions have therefore been taken into account. Direct contracts can be awarded as an emergency measure in the event of interruption or imminent danger to the provision of a service and the duration of such contracts may not exceed two years. According to Article 5 (4) of the Regulation, it is possible to conclude a direct contract where it concerns the provision of public passenger transport services of less than 300000 km per year. Secondly, the Public Transport Act is complemented by the regulation of liner licences in water traffic, as it makes it possible to better regulate the operation of regular passenger transport on commercial grounds and to distinguish commercial journeys from public service journeys. According to the regulation, domestic regular services in water traffic can be operated either on the basis of a regular service permit or a public service contract. This ensures that the State has an overview of all persons operating the regular service. If the State considers it necessary, a public contract shall be awarded in order to find the carrier on a particular route and to award him a public service contract. If the State has not tendered or awarded a public service contract for the operation of a particular route, the person shall have the possibility to operate a regular service on the basis of a regular service licence. Exceptions shall be made when a path permit is not required. Where the carriage of passengers is carried out on an individual basis, the application for a line authorisation is not justified as it is not a regular route. It is also not appropriate to require that carriers operate a ship carrying up to 12 passengers at a time. In that case, it is not a passenger ship. The regulation of the operating licence is planned to enter into force on 1 January 2016. Regulation of line authorisations is necessary in order to ensure that, on publicly supported routes, carriers operating on a commercial basis without the knowledge of the State may arise alongside a carrier performing passenger transport under a public service contract. By offering a service under other conditions and uncontrolled conditions, this could significantly jeopardise the ticket revenues of an existing public regular service and could result in the need to increase public support unexpectedly. In particular, the risk of non-controlled commercial transport is more evident during the peak season if the number of passengers is the highest and all possible ticket revenues as well. In view of the budgetary planning process, such a situation could, on the one hand, undermine the balance of the state budget and make the entire long-term budgetary strategy inadequate. In summary, this situation may jeopardise the stable operation of ferry connections in the long term. In order to control commercial transport, the state currently does not have a mechanism to ensure regular service and freedom of movement for people. A commercial carrier can theoretically one day no longer travel and leave people in trouble. Thus, there may be a risk that people will not be able to get to the island or leave the island. According to line regulation, this option disappears and the carrier is obliged to operate the route on a fixed schedule. On the basis of the line authorisations, commercial shipping journeys would be carried out more systematically, prudently, taking into account all existing conditions and interactions. This is a preventive measure where the State takes the opportunity, not an obligation to intervene in the organisation of regular services, if necessary, in order to ensure that passenger needs and demand are taken into account. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U546	No further questions. Thank you very much to the Minister. I would like to speak with Jaan Õunapuu, member of the Committee on Economic Affairs.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U547	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Dear Minister, The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the draft at its regular meeting on Monday, 8 December. Minister Urve Palo, Director of the Maritime Service of the Aviation and Maritime Department of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, Merily Must, and Head of the Legal Department, Eva Vanamb, had been invited to the meeting and gave an overview of the objectives and content of the draft and answered the questions. This debate, which has been going on for almost an hour, highlighted in many respects all that we discussed in the Commission on 8 December, but since I, as rapporteur for the Commission, have to mark the topics which the Commission discussed, I will do so. I assure you that there was a lively debate in the Commission, where a number of questions and opinions were put. Let's start over. Attention was drawn to the fact that emergency regulation falls within the remit of the Ministry of the Interior. It was asked whether the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications had taken into account the comments made by the Ministry of Interior in the preparation of the draft. The answer was that the Ministry of the Interior disagreed with the coordination round, noting that the existing law, i.e. the General Part of the Code of Economic Activities, would suffice. The main distinction in the understanding of the two ministries is that the TÜS requires expropriation in the event of a disruption of the service of general interest, but under the Emergency Act expropriation is not necessary as there is a right to impose an obligation for a certain period. It must be said that rail traffic and the maintenance of the country's main and supporting roads are currently also regulated by the Emergency Act, but ferry traffic is unfortunately not. There was an interest in the provision of financial arrangements in the event of forced use. It was said that the rules of the standard service of general interest would apply first. The compensation for compulsory use is the same as for any public service compensation, i.e. the cost-based remuneration plus a reasonable profit. Who will pay for the breach of the contract if the carrier has another contract for the provision of the service in another country? The answer was that a certified cost would have to be paid according to the law and that it would also be possible to claim loss of income, but that they would have to be viewed separately depending on the circumstances. At international level, there are certain criteria applying to monopolies that take into account which profits are reasonable. The Competition Authority will not set a single reasonable profit rate, based on the practice of the Competition Authority and other countries to assess which profit is reasonable in a given situation. Now perhaps the most passionate subject, the subject of commercial transport. It was pointed out that the regulation of line authorisations was to be added that, if the carrier has entered into an obligation to carry out a commercial transport operation, he must in any case make planned journeys. Of course, it sounded like the inhabitants of the islands must be guaranteed a high-quality year-round service. The chairman of the committee, Mr Aas, asked about the restriction of competition, which has been mentioned several times in this Chamber today. The Ministry's answer was that if there was an offer that promised better quality, there would be no grounds for refusing to grant a line permit. And as the Minister has confirmed, and we were told in the Commission, that the Ministry is constantly monitoring the ship's occupancy on the basis of ship logs, and that, as appropriate, the state has also ordered more trips to meet demand. There was a question of what implementation and implementation of the forced order would look like in practice and what an emergency could be. The answer was that an emergency is, for example, when ferry connections are cut for a long time so that essential services and goods are available and the island's economic activities are paralyzed. If such a situation arises, an emergency situation will be declared and the State will take the necessary funds into compulsory use. Forced use is by its nature an administrative procedure. In case of forced use, it is not mandatory to ensure the former quality of the standard service, the aim being to resolve the emergency. Kalle Palling asked whether the cancellation of travel in aviation was regulated in the same way as the disruption of ferry traffic. The answer was that aviation has contractual terms and conditions on which the flight may be cancelled. A trip cancelled due to weather conditions is justified in both aviation and ferry traffic, but in the case of ferry connections, the trip must not be cancelled because it has not been sold at 50% or 100%. In case the carrier accidentally adds more trips at a time when there are no queues, he will withdraw the ticket revenue from the state, as has already been said today. Another question was asked which grounds the new law would apply to, is it only to the foundations registered in Estonia? The answer was that the requirement for registration in Estonia has not been included in the Act and in the event of an emergency, forced use will apply to all vehicles located on the territory of Estonia or in Estonian waters. However, the quality of the service does not have to be exactly the same as before, as I have already said. The last issue now: as a service of general interest within the meaning of the TsÜS, the right to request the continuation of the current service is prescribed to be nine months under the same conditions. In the event of a risk of disruption of economic activity, would this not be sufficient time? Answer: An injunction under the CFP may not be a sufficiently effective coercion; a more effective instrument is needed. At the same time, according to the draft, the State has the right to adopt a means of resolving or mitigating a real emergency and the emergency cannot arise from non-compliance with the precepts, but only from the specific consequences for the inhabitants of the islands. It was also expressed the view that a new law was necessary and it was noted that changes to the three laws should have been made earlier. It was also recalled that there was a risk of disruption of ferry traffic as early as 2006, when the public procurements carried out failed. The Commission decided to put the draft on the plenary agenda by 17 December 2014, i.e. today, to propose that the first reading be closed and that the deadline for amendments be set at 12 noon on 5 January 2015. All these decisions were consensual. Thank you very much.

The commission's proposal was to conclude the first reading of draft 807. First reading is over. I will set the deadline for submitting amendments to the draft at 12 noon on 5 January 2015.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U549	Our 20th item today is the first reading of the draft Act amending the Law on Explosives initiated by the Government of the Republic. Once again, I would like to ask for a presentation of the Chair's Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, Urve Palo!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U550	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good ambassadors! I hope you can get rid of me soon, I've been here a long time. The draft law amending the Explosives Act transposes into Estonian law Directive 2013/29/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council on the harmonisation of the laws of the Member States relating to the making available on the market of pyrotechnic articles. The Directive has the effect of clarifying the obligations of manufacturers, importers and distributors of pyrotechnic articles in the Explosives Act. I will bring out three major changes. First, economic operators are obliged to keep for 10 years information on an economic operator who has supplied them with a pyrotechnic article or who has supplied it with a pyrotechnic article. The current regulation also required economic operators to keep this information, but it was not specified for how long. The amendment is necessary to ensure better traceability so that, in cases where a product proves to be non-compliant or dangerous, it is possible to locate such a product and, where appropriate, inform consumers or recover the product from the consumer. Operators need not start keeping a separate register or records for this purpose if the information concerned is, for example, found on delivery notes which are kept for a sufficient period of time. Secondly, categories I, II, III and IV of pyrotechnic articles are replaced by the symbols F1, F2, F3 and F4 respectively. Star F comes from the word fireworks. The amendment will ensure better differentiation from other categories of pyrotechnic articles. Thirdly, pyrotechnic articles of category P1 intended for vehicles, including airbag and belt pre-loading systems, are not made available to the consumer. These are products for technical purposes which may contain explosive explosive substances and which are required by manufacturers of cars or their safety devices or by other professional users who repair or change such safety equipment. The essential requirements for pyrotechnic articles shall not be altered. The effect of the change. The impact of the draft on the administrative burden on economic operators and on day-to-day activities is minimal. The draft is planned to enter into force on 1 July 2015 and is due to Directive 2013/29/EU. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U554	Our next item today, item 21, is the first reading of the draft Fisheries Act 801 initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask for a presentation of the speech chair, Minister of Environment Mati Raidma!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U555	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, At the end of the day, five drafts from the environmental field. First of all, the draft law on fishing. The main objective of the draft Fisheries Act, which was prepared for you at the Ministry of the Environment, is to facilitate the understanding of regulation. The procedure of the Act was initiated within the framework of the codification of the Environmental Code. One of the objectives of codification is to harmonise the internal structure of the chapters of environmental law. The current Law on fishing has been amended several times, its structure has become very cumbersome and it undermines its comprehension of substance. The linguistic changes made to the law now ensure a better understanding. Some substantive changes have also been made which will improve the possibilities for buying and selling fish for own use and add a new fishing gear to recreational fishing gear. The possibility of selling fish caught by-catch is increased, and fishing in coastal fisheries can take place irrespective of the depth of the sea. Increased monitoring possibilities for detecting infringements, carrying out procedures and prosecuting them. According to the draft, a professional fisherman may sell the fish he catches to a natural person without a first buy-in receipt and the natural person may buy the fish for his own use up to 30 kilograms per day. According to the current law, the cost of the quantity of fish purchased for own use must not exceed EUR 19,2 per day. At present, the threshold for buying fish for own use is too low for noble fish such as salmon, trout, eel, herring, and eye. Thus, no salmon can be purchased for own use, as the weight of a single specimen is usually 5 kilograms and the cost exceeds EUR 19,2. It is not appropriate to rely on the amount of money, but on the amount of fish that can be up to 30 kilograms as a result of a European Union regulation, in the regulation on the purchase and sale of fish for own use. The proposal includes a kadiska among recreational fishing gear. Kadiska is an environmentally friendly trap with no fishing capacity and which can be discarded alive. According to the draft, one person may be allowed to use up to three fishing gears for recreational fisheries on one or more waters where fishing opportunities are established. In particular, fishing with Kadiska should be permitted in relatively small waters where there is no possibility of gillnet fishing and where the stock is generally underused, for example in Estonia for the most common species of less valuable fish, such as shirt, perch, nurg, flax, etc. Fishing opportunities shall be established on the basis of scientific advice. The law has been supplemented in the light of the provisions of the EU regulations on the ban on discards. It shall be prohibited to discard fish of species subject to the Union ban on discards, such as sprat, herring, cod and salmon, excluding fish caught in recreational fisheries using fishing gears. For the remaining species, the current system will continue. Unviable fish are not allowed to be discarded, but viable fish must be released. It is also necessary to recover fish caught in recreational fisheries by fishing gear, as in most cases such fish survive. At present, there are quite frequent situations where fishing undersized fish is justified by the ban on discarding unviable fish, despite the fact that the fish is viable and should be discarded. The change should improve the situation. The sale/purchase provision for fish caught as by-catch allowing the sale of sized fish caught as by-catch has been supplemented. The possibilities of selling undersized fish have also been improved, allowing fish caught for scientific purposes and fish undersized fish subject to EU discard ban to be sold for non-human consumption, such as animal feed, fertiliser production, cosmetics, etc. Undersized fish for human consumption shall be prohibited in the European Union. This provision enters into force on 1 January 2016, a transitional period is necessary to comply with the fishing rules. The law is supplemented by a GPS tracking device that is valid on Lake Peipsi, Lämmi- ja Pihkva. All vessels engaged in commercial fishing must be equipped with GPS. Currently, since 2012, this requirement applies only to vessels equipped with bottom seines fishing on Lake Peipsi. These waters have major problems with respect to compliance and it is therefore essential to strengthen surveillance. In the light of international experience, the installation of surveillance systems on means of transport is considered cost-effective and has justified itself in the conservation of fish stocks. On board vessels equipped with bottom seines, GPS data has proved to be very informative and necessary, in particular to improve environmental inspections. At present, lakes are in a situation where fishing is carried out with a large number of unmarked and allegedly stolen tagged gears. Tracking systems show which vessels operate in the area and consequently measures can be taken to prevent and detect further infringements. GPS equipment must be installed on some 350 vessels, the costs are planned to be covered by the Environmental Investment Centre. The amendment will enter into force on 1 September 2015, a transitional period is necessary for the execution of the relevant procurements and the purchase of equipment and the installation of professional fishing vessels on border lakes. At the level of the law, the concept of damage to fish stocks is laid down, which until now is regulated in the implementing act. For the purposes of the current Regulation, damage to a stock is to cause damage to fish by killing, injuring, otherwise destroying or removing it from the natural environment, where such action results in a breach of the requirements of law or other legislation. This definition is very general. The draft provides for specific cases of non-compliance at the level of the law where the damage has been caused. It is foreseen that fishing without the required fishing rights, fishing for undersized fish, fishing during and at a prohibited time, fishing for a prohibited species and non-compliance with the fishing gear requirements will cause damage to the stocks. It is also foreseen as an innovation that fish stocks will be harmed even if they are sold, bought or processed and the origin of fish has not been proven and if they are sold undersized fish. In practice, there are rare cases where fish that are undersized or for which there is no evidence of origin are found in the inspection of a fishing establishment. This practice is punishable, but the damage to fish stocks has not been calculated. The illegal purchase, sale and handling of fish directly affects fish stocks, as fish caught illegally are sold at a cheaper price and are more easily found in the market. According to the draft, the number of misdemeanours has increased. The misdemeanours provided for in the fishing law in force so far included all infringements of fishing, conservation or exploitation requirements, which did not give a specific description of the act, i.e. was very general. The draft provides for specific acts the commission of which is punishable. As an amendment, the new law provides for an increase in the maximum penalty of up to EUR 3200 for a misdemeanour in force until now, up to EUR 32000. The current penalty rate has remained unchanged since 2003, but the statistics show that the current level of fines does not make the behaviour of economic operators as necessary to take account of the requirements of the law in an area of activity where the proceeds from illegal fishing can be very high. The penalty rate for natural persons shall not be changed. In essence, I have described all the important changes that the law brings with it. The Act has passed through all mandatory coordination circles and has reached this Riigikogu. Dear Riigikogu, please support the bill. Thank you.

There is clear support from the Ministry of the Environment for this.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U566	To support the position of the Minister and of my colleague Korb, I ask such a question. But if we were to assume that one is responsible for the resource that remains in nature and the other, let's say, production, I honestly think that the interference of the Ministry of Agriculture in this matter is a complete misunderstanding. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U567	There's a clear connection to your first question, to hand over the scientists. Because if they take out the fish, it's like they're more connected to the Ministry of Agriculture, to whom they could address your question in the future.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U568	Any more questions? No further questions. Thank you very much. I would like to ask Annely Akkermann, Member of the Environment Committee, for a co-report.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U569	Good evening, Mr President, and ladies and gentlemen. At the meeting of the Committee on the Environment of the Riigikogu on 9 December, the draft Fisheries Act was examined in 801. Kaire Märtin, Head of the Fish Stocks Department of the Ministry of the Environment, and Helen Holtsman, lawyer at the Legal Department, presented the draft and answered questions from the members of the committee. The draft has been drawn up in the framework of the codification of environmental law and there are few substantive changes to the new law compared to the existing law, but the rules are more logically structured in the interests of comprehension. The Minister gave an adequate overview of the draft. I will take a closer look at some aspects raised by the Commission. The question arose as to why some provisions have a different date from the general entry into force procedure. Kaire Märtin replied that the dates of entry into force were set on the basis of the autumn fishing season in Lake Peipsi, Pihkva and Lämmijärv and the desire to leave the target group more time to familiarise themselves with the new conditions of the law. The introduction of an electronic marking requirement for fishing gear was requested. The current legal framework allows for the introduction of electronic labels. The idea has been working for a year. There is an overview of the technical solutions and their cost, and the development of the fisheries information system to implement the electronic marking of fishing gear. Both the electronic marking of fishing gear and the requirement to have GPS equipment on board fishing vessels serve the objective of reducing illegal fishing. At present, only the fisherman to whom the fishing authorisation has been issued may fish with the net. It was proposed to consider an amendment that, if, for example, a fisherman is ill, he could be replaced by another fisherman in the removal of the gillnet. It also recommended that the authorisation for professional fishing be made electronic, as is the authorisation for recreational fishing. Kaire Märtin replied that professional fishermen themselves should be prepared for the introduction of electronic licences, but they are not yet. Concerns were expressed about the high levels of fines imposed on individual fishermen. The fines should be capped so that the fine does not call illegal fishermen into order, but does not completely ruin the fishermen's families. The amount of the fine should be reasonable. The Fish Damage Regulation will be reviewed and a ceiling will also be set. In the first half of the following year, by-catch rates shall be reviewed and a provision shall enter into force that fish caught by-catch shall be allowed to be sold. The by-catch rate for gillnets shall be 8% of the numerical quantity. A question was asked about the size of the eye of the fishing gear and the so-called double criterion for the size of the fish caught. Both will remain in force in parallel, and both minimum mesh size and minimum fish size will continue to be established. An important amendment is based on the EU regulation, which was already mentioned here. A private individual has the right to buy 30 kg of fish a day for personal consumption, and so far this quantity was too small. The provision regulates so-called boat fishing that it is not necessary to go to the first buyer to buy fish. Recreational fishermen have raised the issue that some, not the most honest among them, fish much more than they consume tens and hundreds of kilos a day. Recreational fishermen are prohibited from selling fish, but large quantities of fish caught trigger a chain of illegal activities and undermine both the stock and fair competition in professional fishing. The proposal to supplement the law was discussed in such a way that a recreational fisherman should not fish more than 30 kilos a day. Even if it is difficult to control, the law must state that catching more than 30 kilograms is illegal. Professional fishing is very strictly regulated, effective monitoring has been established and considerable fines have been imposed. It is unfair to professional fishermen that recreational fishermen can try much more than they can consume and benefit from illegal activities, with virtually no way of sanctioning them. Both measures and monitoring were found to be necessary to control illegal recreational fishing. The Committee on the Environment set the deadline for the amendments to be made slightly beyond what was normal in order to allow time for the organisation of a seminar for recreational and professional fishermen. The desire is to obtain certainty from fishermen about the comprehensibility and practical applicability of the law and to propose amendments which have been discussed with them. The Committee on the Environment decided to propose to the Board of the Riigikogu that the draft Fisheries Act 801 be sent to the first reading today, 17 December 2014, proposing that the first reading be completed and that the deadline for submitting amendments to the draft be set at 16 a.m. on 15 January 2015. It was also decided to appoint a rapporteur for the draft here. Thank you so much for listening.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U571	Our next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the law on ambient air protection initiated by the Government of the Republic. On behalf of the initiator, the Minister of the Environment Mati Raidma will make a presentation. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U572	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Next is a draft amendment to the law on ambient air protection prepared at the Ministry of the Environment. Firstly, we wish to have a new greenhouse gas emissions trading scheme in Estonia in accordance with the draft law. The new greenhouse gas emissions trading scheme will operate from 2005 alongside the European Union's greenhouse gas emission trading scheme (ETS) for the period 2013 to 2020. This concerns sectors outside the EU trading system, transport, agriculture, waste management, small-scale or up to 20 MW power generation, as well as energy savings in buildings. While the functioning of the trading system imposes greenhouse gas emission limitation obligations on the largest companies in the energy production and industry, the new scheme operates on other bases. Emission limitation obligations are imposed on Member States. In order to fulfil its greenhouse gas limitation obligation more flexibly and cost-effectively, a country has the possibility to trade new credits. The trading of new units will take place on the basis of the same principles as the functioning of AAU emission allowance trading, in which Estonia has been quite successful in its activities. Secondly, we want to ensure the functioning of the European Union's greenhouse gas emission allowance trading system in Estonia, in accordance with the draft law. In addition to large-scale industry and energy production, aviation is also part of the EU's trading system. The European Commission has asked us, in connection with this directive, to supplement Estonian laws, mainly to improve wording and to improve terminology. These changes do not impose any additional obligations on operators of aircraft operated by Estonia. In addition to the above, the draft introduces technical changes to the legislation on fluorinated greenhouse gases (F-gases). Again, the changes to the new EU regulation on fluorinated greenhouse gases are mainly technical terms, requirements for the labelling of equipment and the registration of equipment and maintenance operations. As the environmental risks of fluorinated greenhouse gases vary, the substance itself will be taken into account in the future and its quantity will have to be converted into carbon dioxide equivalent. Honourable Riigikogu, I ask you to support the draft.

According to the introduction presented to us, the bill contains three basic things that will be changed. Firstly, the Aviation Directive is being fully transposed. Secondly, the aim is to create a new quota trading system. While the country has so far traded with the so-called new ones, since 2013, in addition to the European Union's trading system, each Member State of the European Union has set its own greenhouse gas emission limits. This system includes smaller heat and power generators (up to 20 MW), the transport sector, the waste sector and agriculture. In these areas, commitments are foreseen for the country up to 2030, i.e. the obligation to limit the growth of greenhouse gas emissions. And if the country is able to keep emissions below the limit, it will be possible to sell excess quotas to other countries. According to plans and forecasts, Estonia has three million units of surplus quota that could be sold by 2020. The third block of amendments concerns the FFC Regulation, which is scheduled to enter into force on 1 January next year. We learned that the most critical is the transport sector, that there might be a shortage. Thirdly, the question was whether there might be a need to use emission credits in relation to forest management. In response, we have received that, according to current knowledge, the likely issue of the forestry sector is such that we can cover it with our units, with our surplus, there is no additional need. And even if the maximum or actually sustainable cutting volume of 15 million cubic metres is achieved after the Forest Management Development Plan, if there is a need for an additional mission, the European Commission has promised that it will help Estonia to solve such problems. ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U576	The next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Waste Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. The initiator's waitress is Minister of the Environment Mati Raidma. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U577	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! You have received a draft law amending the Waste Act prepared at the Ministry of the Environment. It clarifies the provisions on the removal of waste batteries and accumulators from electrical and electronic equipment in the Waste Act. The need to amend the Waste Act is due to the transposition into Estonian law of the amendments to the Battery Directive No 2013/56/EU. The amendments to the Directive must be transposed by 1 July 2015. The law amending the draft Waste Act complements one section of the current Waste Act concerning the manufacturer's obligations in the manufacture of electrical and electronic equipment containing batteries and accumulators. The current Waste Act obliges manufacturers of electrical and electronic equipment containing batteries and accumulators to design their equipment in such a way that waste batteries and accumulators can be easily removed from the equipment. The transposition of the amendments to the Battery Directive clarifies the obligations of manufacturers, adding that manufacturers must also provide for the possibility for a qualified worker independent of the manufacturer to be able to remove waste batteries and accumulators from the plant when designing the equipment. With electrical and electronic equipment containing batteries and batteries, the manufacturer must provide instructions explaining how to safely remove waste batteries or accumulators. A new addition must be added to the instructions, explaining to the end user or qualified staff how to remove waste batteries and accumulators from the equipment. In conclusion, I note that the implementation of the draft law will have a positive impact on the environment, as it will be possible to extend the lifetime of electrical and electronic equipment. Where an EEE user has a device in which he cannot change the battery or the battery himself, and he is only considering giving up his EEE, he or she shall have the possibility, if he or she so wishes, to have a qualified worker independent of the manufacturer replace the battery or battery in his or her EEE. In that case, he has no need to abandon his device or buy a new one. The proposed change will thus contribute to reducing the consumption of natural resources, as fewer new products will have to be produced and the amount of waste generated by the products will also be reduced. Honourable Riigikogu! I would also ask you to support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U584	Our next item is another draft law on the amendment of the Waste Act initiated by the Government of the Republic, this time at 790. This is a first reading, and the waitress is Minister of the Environment Mati Raidma. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U585	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to introduce you to the next draft law amending the Waste Act, which, unlike the one on the previous item on the agenda, seeks to solve our own problems and does not directly relate to the directives of the European Union. The content of the draft is to lay down in the Waste Act as a special measure of national supervision a control operation which is an effective measure to control the activities of the purchasers of metal waste. A control transaction means an offer to a collector of metal for the purchase of prohibited metal waste and, in the event of acceptance, the execution of a disposal transaction and fixing that the purchaser does not comply with the special conditions laid down in the Waste Act. Specific conditions are laid down for the collection of metal waste. The purchase of metal objects of apparent artistic or historical value as waste is prohibited. Buying-in of electric wires and cables, road signs and signposts, underground communication wells, metal shutters, railway tracks, motor vehicles or their essential parts as waste is only allowed from companies that have a corresponding licence, such as road maintenance licences. When purchasing the abovementioned metal waste, it must also be observed that the purchase cannot be paid in cash and that a document must be drawn up concerning the purchase. According to the bill, the control operation is not required to be presented or to wear uniforms by the control official when the transaction is made. The official shall also not be required to produce a certificate of formality until the purpose of the check has been achieved. The inspection operation shall be decided by the Director-General of the Environmental Inspectorate or by an official authorised by him. The Environmental Inspectorate and the Police and Border Guard Board have significantly enhanced cooperation in this area in recent years. A cooperation agreement has been concluded according to which the two institutions will carry out joint controls across Estonia with metal waste collectors. Nevertheless, metal thefts from substations and railways, as well as thefts from manholeholes, pose a serious risk, disrupting the functioning of critical infrastructure and also endangering human lives. Thefts are carried out primarily for the purpose of obtaining money from the sale of an electric cable or a metal hatch. The amendment of the law also has a preventive character in relation to a possible offence. If scrap collectors know that an inspector has the right to carry out control sales and do not know whether or not the person offering metal waste to them is from the Environmental Inspectorate, it is possible that the purchase of restricted metal waste will be refused more frequently. I therefore note, in conclusion, that this is an important bill in our circumstances. Honourable Riigikogu, I ask you to support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U598	Our next item is the first reading of the Environmental Impact Assessment and Environmental Management System Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and, in this connection, the draft law amending other laws. The initiator's waitress is Minister of the Environment Mati Raidma. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-12-17_U599	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Last but not least, let me present the draft law on environmental impact assessment prepared by the Ministry of the Environment and the amendment of the Environmental Management System Act and, in this connection, the amendment of other laws. The current law has been in force for nearly ten years and during this period only minor changes have been made to the legislation. There is therefore a practical need to correct and supplement the system as a whole. The aim of the draft is to make the impact assessment process more effective, clearer and transparent and of higher quality as a whole. Environmental impact assessments shall be carried out in the course of the licensing process. Strategic Environmental Impact Assessments will be carried out during the preparation of national development plans and spatial plans. The grantor of an authorisation or the organiser of the preparation of a planning document, the decision-maker of a single name is different bodies, such as the Environmental Board and the Ministry of the Environment, mostly local authorities in case of construction permits or plans. In both cases, the main objective is to provide the decision-maker with the necessary information for the authorisation or the establishment of a planning document. One of the main changes to the draft law is the decision-maker's increased involvement in the process. It will be given a leading role in the impact assessment process, which means carrying out the entire procedure and contributing substantially. In this way, the decisions will take better account of the results of the impact assessment. Another important change will be the question of the authorities' views on the content and documentation of the impact assessment, based on the area of competence. This will take place at all important stages: identifying the need to initiate an impact assessment, preparing a programme or initial task, and reporting as a final result. It is important to point out that the report also needs to be coordinated with the relevant authorities. The full involvement of the institutions will extend the duration of the procedure, but it will have an important role to play. This will only trigger an impact assessment in justified cases and ensure the quality of the impact assessment. With wide-ranging involvement, the decision-maker receives feedback, which is the basis for better and more transparent decision-making. Consultation and closer cooperation between the parties will also help to avoid possible conflicts at later stages, including from a public perspective. The quality of the impact assessment depends to a large extent on the competence of the experts. Therefore, the draft has given important attention to expert-related aspects. The draft sets out an approach based on a lead expert and clarifies and strengthens its competence requirements. For example, a leading expert on environmental impact assessment will need to have a Master's degree and five years of work experience in the future and have completed 60 hours of impact assessment management training. A leading expert on Strategic Environmental Impact Assessment (SEA) will be subject to similar competence requirements. The requirement that a strategic environmental assessment of a detailed plan may only be carried out by a leading expert with an environmental impact assessment licence can be specifically mentioned. In the implementation of the Act, the grounds for the revocation of the environmental impact assessment expert's licence have emerged as a defect, which is why the draft clarifies the rules governing the withdrawal of the licence. The draft also specifies and corrects the list of mandatory environmental impact assessment activities. The list of documents covered by the Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA) will also be structured. This will make it easier for decision-makers to understand, in which case the assessment must be initiated on a mandatory basis and when consideration of the initiation of the assessment is required. It can also be pointed out that changes to the draft strategic environmental assessment are related to the planning law pending in the Riigikogu. According to the draft, the planning law will continue to apply to the Strategic Environmental Assessment of Plans. On the substantive side of the Strategic Environmental Assessment, the Environmental Impact Assessment and Environmental Management System Act will continue to be regulated. The draft also brings the law into line with the General Part of the Environmental Code by harmonising the terminology of the laws. Honourable Riigikogu, I ask you to support the draft. Thank you.
